Um… mostly work, I guess. Things have been getting bad enough there that people noticed, and it scared them. I’m a waitress, and during service I’m usually moving fast, you know, closing checks, running drinks, remembering table numbers—just normal stuff. But lately I’ve been sort of… freezing up in the middle of it.

Like I’ll be standing by the soda station and I’m supposed to be doing one thing, but I’ll have the cash drawer key in my hand and I’ll just be holding it, not using it, and I’ll start repeating table numbers out loud instead of actually closing the checks. Or I’ll do things in the wrong order, like my brain skips the part that tells me what comes next. It’s embarrassing, but more than that, people at work got worried because I’d just be stuck there while everything’s busy around me. That’s why I came in.

Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it “hopeless” exactly, but I’ve definitely stopped doing things. Like I used to go for walks on the beach, not even as a big thing, just to clear my head after work or on a day off. And I used to bake sometimes, or mess around in the garden, or write in my journal when I had too much in my head. Those were sort of my things.

But now it’s like… I just don’t start them. I’ll think, “I should go outside,” or “I could bake something,” and then I just sit there. It’s not even always sadness, it’s more like there’s no push behind me. My husband will try to talk with me, like ask what I want to do or how I’m feeling, and I mostly just sit there and give him little answers. I can tell he’s trying, and I know I should care more in the moment, but it feels like I can’t get myself to care or get moving.

I’m not really sure. I don’t know that I’ve noticed it as being tired exactly. I mean, there are days when I sit around and don’t get much done, but I don’t always feel like, “Oh, I’m exhausted.” It’s more… I don’t know how to explain the difference.

It’s hard for me to separate that from everything else.

I’ll think, “I should go outside,” or “I could bake something,” and then I just sit there. It’s not even always sadness, it’s more like there’s no push behind me. My husband will try to talk with me, like ask what I want to do or how I’m feeling, and I mostly just sit there and give him little answers. I can tell he’s trying, and I know I should care more in the moment, but it feels like I can’t get myself to care or get moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel more tired or that you’ve lost energy, even when you’re not doing much? Like, even after resting or on days when you haven’t done anything physically hard?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t know that I’ve noticed it as being tired exactly. I mean, there are days when I sit around and don’t get much done, but I don’t always feel like, “Oh, I’m exhausted.” It’s more… I don’t know how to explain the difference.\n\nIf I rest, I’m still kind of the same afterward, but I can’t tell if that’s tiredness or just me not getting started. Like I’ll be on the couch for a while and then think I should get up and do something, and I just don’t. But I wouldn’t say I feel physically worn out all the time, not in a clear way. It’s hard for me to separate that from everything else.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, not just occasionally or in response to specific things happening?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’m not sure I can say that, exactly. I wouldn’t want to say I’m depressed most of the day every day, because that sounds very specific, and I don’t know that I track it that clearly.

There are definitely times where I feel flat, or like I’m sitting under something heavy, but I don’t always feel sad in the way I think people mean when they say depressed. Sometimes I’m just… kind of blank. And then other times something will happen, like my husband asking me what I want for dinner or whether I want to go out, and I realize I don’t really have an answer, but I don’t know if that’s depressed or just me being stuck.

So I guess… I don’t know. I’ve noticed I’m not myself, but I can’t honestly say it’s a constant down feeling nearly every day. It’s more mixed up than that.

Like I’ll be on the couch for a while and then think I should get up and do something, and I just don’t. But I wouldn’t say I feel physically worn out all the time, not in a clear way. It’s hard for me to separate that from everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, not just occasionally or in response to specific things happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, exactly. I wouldn’t want to say I’m depressed most of the day every day, because that sounds very specific, and I don’t know that I track it that clearly.\n\nThere are definitely times where I feel flat, or like I’m sitting under something heavy, but I don’t always feel sad in the way I think people mean when they say depressed. Sometimes I’m just… kind of blank. And then other times something will happen, like my husband asking me what I want for dinner or whether I want to go out, and I realize I don’t really have an answer, but I don’t know if that’s depressed or just me being stuck.\n\nSo I guess… I don’t know. I’ve noticed I’m not myself, but I can’t honestly say it’s a constant down feeling nearly every day. It’s more mixed up than that.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that activities you used to enjoy, like walking on the beach or baking, don’t feel as rewarding or pleasurable as they used to?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Negative_Symptoms\nmanifestation: \"I don’t really go for beach walks, bake, garden, or write in my journal much anymore, and when my husband tries to talk with me I mostly just sit there because it feels like I can’t get myself to care or start anything.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. It’s hard to say if they don’t feel good anymore because I barely even get to the point of doing them, you know? Like the beach walks — I used to like that feeling of just being outside and hearing the water and kind of letting my head settle. And baking, too, it used to feel kind of satisfying, like there was a reason to start and then something nice at the end.

But lately I just… don’t really do any of it. I don’t go for walks much, I don’t bake, I don’t really garden, and I haven’t been writing in my journal like I used to. I’ll think about it sometimes, like, “That would probably be good for me,” but then it feels like there’s no spark or pull to actually start. It’s not like I’m actively thinking, “I hate this now.” It’s more like it doesn’t reach me the same way.

And when my husband tries to talk to me or encourage me, I mostly just sit there. I can hear him, and I know he’s trying to help, but I give these short answers and I don’t really engage. It feels like I can’t make myself care enough to get moving, even about things I know I used to enjoy.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess when I do them… or, really, when I think about doing them, they feel kind of flat now. It’s hard to answer because I don’t actually get myself to do them much anymore. Like beach walks used to be something I would just go do, even if it was just for a little while, and I’d feel better after. Baking used to feel like there was a point to it, like I could focus on the steps and then have something nice at the end.

Now it’s more like I think, “I could bake,” or “I should go outside,” and then I just sit there. I don’t really garden either, and I haven’t been writing in my journal much. It’s not that I do those things and hate them, exactly. It’s more like I can’t get the feeling started, if that makes sense. The satisfying part doesn’t seem strong enough to pull me toward it.

And even with my husband, when he tries to talk with me or get me to do something, I mostly just sit there and give little answers. I know he’s trying, and I know these are things I used to care about, but it feels like I can’t make myself care enough to actually start.

I’m a waitress, and during service I’m usually moving fast, you know, closing checks, running drinks, remembering table numbers—just normal stuff. But lately I’ve been sort of… freezing up in the middle of it.\n\nLike I’ll be standing by the soda station and I’m supposed to be doing one thing, but I’ll have the cash drawer key in my hand and I’ll just be holding it, not using it, and I’ll start repeating table numbers out loud instead of actually closing the checks. Or I’ll do things in the wrong order, like my brain skips the part that tells me what comes next. It’s embarrassing, but more than that, people at work got worried because I’d just be stuck there while everything’s busy around me. That’s why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you usually enjoy, especially around the time these work issues started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it “hopeless” exactly, but I’ve definitely stopped doing things. Like I used to go for walks on the beach, not even as a big thing, just to clear my head after work or on a day off. And I used to bake sometimes, or mess around in the garden, or write in my journal when I had too much in my head. Those were sort of my things.\n\nBut now it’s like… I just don’t start them. I’ll think, “I should go outside,” or “I could bake something,” and then I just sit there. It’s not even always sadness, it’s more like there’s no push behind me. My husband will try to talk with me, like ask what I want to do or how I’m feeling, and I mostly just sit there and give him little answers. I can tell he’s trying, and I know I should care more in the moment, but it feels like I can’t get myself to care or get moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel more tired or that you’ve lost energy, even when you’re not doing much? Like, even after resting or on days when you haven’t done anything physically hard?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t know that I’ve noticed it as being tired exactly. I mean, there are days when I sit around and don’t get much done, but I don’t always feel like, “Oh, I’m exhausted.” It’s more… I don’t know how to explain the difference.\n\nIf I rest, I’m still kind of the same afterward, but I can’t tell if that’s tiredness or just me not getting started. Like I’ll be on the couch for a while and then think I should get up and do something, and I just don’t. But I wouldn’t say I feel physically worn out all the time, not in a clear way. It’s hard for me to separate that from everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, not just occasionally or in response to specific things happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, exactly. I wouldn’t want to say I’m depressed most of the day every day, because that sounds very specific, and I don’t know that I track it that clearly.\n\nThere are definitely times where I feel flat, or like I’m sitting under something heavy, but I don’t always feel sad in the way I think people mean when they say depressed. Sometimes I’m just… kind of blank. And then other times something will happen, like my husband asking me what I want for dinner or whether I want to go out, and I realize I don’t really have an answer, but I don’t know if that’s depressed or just me being stuck.\n\nSo I guess… I don’t know. I’ll think about it sometimes, like, “That would probably be good for me,” but then it feels like there’s no spark or pull to actually start. It’s not like I’m actively thinking, “I hate this now.” It’s more like it doesn’t reach me the same way.\n\nAnd when my husband tries to talk to me or encourage me, I mostly just sit there. I can hear him, and I know he’s trying to help, but I give these short answers and I don’t really engage. It feels like I can’t make myself care enough to get moving, even about things I know I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the things you used to enjoy, like walking on the beach or baking, do they still feel good or satisfying when you do them, or do they feel more flat or empty now?\n\nPatient: I guess when I do them… or, really, when I think about doing them, they feel kind of flat now. It’s hard to answer because I don’t actually get myself to do them much anymore. Like beach walks used to be something I would just go do, even if it was just for a little while, and I’d feel better after. Baking used to feel like there was a point to it, like I could focus on the steps and then have something nice at the end.\n\nNow it’s more like I think, “I could bake,” or “I should go outside,” and then I just sit there. I don’t really garden either, and I haven’t been writing in my journal much. It’s not that I do those things and hate them, exactly. It’s more like I can’t get the feeling started, if that makes sense. The satisfying part doesn’t seem strong enough to pull me toward it.\n\nAnd even with my husband, when he tries to talk with me or get me to do something, I mostly just sit there and give little answers. I know he’s trying, and I know these are things I used to care about, but it feels like I can’t make myself care enough to actually start.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the day, even when nothing specific has happened to make you feel that way?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say that, exactly. “Most of the day, most days” feels hard for me to answer because I’m not really measuring it that way in my head.

It’s not like I wake up and think, “I’m depressed,” and then feel that same sadness all day. Sometimes I feel heavy or kind of dull, like everything takes more effort than it should, but other times it’s more blank than sad. And sometimes I only notice something’s off when there’s no real reason for me to be sitting there doing nothing, but I still can’t seem to move into the day.

So… I don’t want to say yes if that means a clear depressed mood all day when nothing happened. I’m just not sure. I know I haven’t felt like myself, and a lot of days feel flat, but I can’t honestly pin it down as being down or depressed for most of the day nearly every day.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes, I think so. It’s not just like forgetting where I put my phone or something normal like that. At work especially, it’s been… really embarrassing, and honestly it scared people, I think.

I’m a waitress, so usually I can keep track of what I’m doing — table numbers, checks, who needs drinks, all that. But lately I’ll get stuck in the middle of service. Like I’ll be standing by the soda station with the cash drawer key in my hand, and instead of closing out checks or doing the next step, I’ll just kind of freeze there. And I’ll be saying table numbers under my breath, or repeating them, like I’m trying to line everything up in my head but it won’t connect.

And it’s not just slow thinking. It’s like I do things out of order. I’ll start one task, then hold something I’m supposed to use for another task, and then I don’t finish either one. People will ask me, “Are you okay?” or tell me a table is waiting, and I know they’re talking to me, but it takes me a second to understand what I’m supposed to do next.

Small decisions feel big in those moments. Like closing a check should be simple — press the right buttons, take the payment, give the receipt — but I’ll just stand there like the steps got scrambled. I hate saying that because I know how to do my job. I’ve done it plenty. But lately my brain just… doesn’t move in the right order sometimes.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it’s more flat now. Or maybe I should say I mostly don’t even get to the point of doing them, which is part of what bothers me.

Like the beach is right there, and I used to be able to just go take a walk and feel at least a little clearer afterward. Baking too — I used to like the steps of it, measuring things out, waiting for it to come together. And gardening, writing in my journal… those were things that felt like mine. Now they just kind of sit there in my mind like chores I can’t start.

When I think about doing them, there isn’t that pull anymore. It’s not like I do them and feel joyless every single time, because honestly I barely do them. It’s more like I can’t get myself to care enough to begin. I’ll think, “Maybe I should bake something,” and then I just sit there, and the thought passes, and nothing happens.

And with my husband, it’s similar in a way. He’ll try to talk to me or get me to do something with him, and I know I should respond more, I know he’s trying, but I mostly just sit there and give little answers. It’s like the feeling that should make me want to connect or start something just doesn’t kick in.

And I used to bake sometimes, or mess around in the garden, or write in my journal when I had too much in my head. Those were sort of my things.\n\nBut now it’s like… I just don’t start them. I’ll think, “I should go outside,” or “I could bake something,” and then I just sit there. It’s not even always sadness, it’s more like there’s no push behind me. My husband will try to talk with me, like ask what I want to do or how I’m feeling, and I mostly just sit there and give him little answers. I can tell he’s trying, and I know I should care more in the moment, but it feels like I can’t get myself to care or get moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel more tired or that you’ve lost energy, even when you’re not doing much? Like, even after resting or on days when you haven’t done anything physically hard?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t know that I’ve noticed it as being tired exactly. I mean, there are days when I sit around and don’t get much done, but I don’t always feel like, “Oh, I’m exhausted.” It’s more… I don’t know how to explain the difference.\n\nIf I rest, I’m still kind of the same afterward, but I can’t tell if that’s tiredness or just me not getting started. Like I’ll be on the couch for a while and then think I should get up and do something, and I just don’t. But I wouldn’t say I feel physically worn out all the time, not in a clear way. It’s hard for me to separate that from everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, not just occasionally or in response to specific things happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, exactly. I wouldn’t want to say I’m depressed most of the day every day, because that sounds very specific, and I don’t know that I track it that clearly.\n\nThere are definitely times where I feel flat, or like I’m sitting under something heavy, but I don’t always feel sad in the way I think people mean when they say depressed. Sometimes I’m just… kind of blank. And then other times something will happen, like my husband asking me what I want for dinner or whether I want to go out, and I realize I don’t really have an answer, but I don’t know if that’s depressed or just me being stuck.\n\nSo I guess… I don’t know. I’ve noticed I’m not myself, but I can’t honestly say it’s a constant down feeling nearly every day. It’s more mixed up than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to enjoy, like walking on the beach or baking, don’t feel as rewarding or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s hard to say if they don’t feel good anymore because I barely even get to the point of doing them, you know? Like the beach walks — I used to like that feeling of just being outside and hearing the water and kind of letting my head settle. And baking, too, it used to feel kind of satisfying, like there was a reason to start and then something nice at the end.\n\nBut lately I just… don’t really do any of it. I don’t go for walks much, I don’t bake, I don’t really garden, and I haven’t been writing in my journal like I used to. I’ll think about it sometimes, like, “That would probably be good for me,” but then it feels like there’s no spark or pull to actually start. It’s not like I’m actively thinking, “I hate this now.” It’s more like it doesn’t reach me the same way.\n\nAnd when my husband tries to talk to me or encourage me, I mostly just sit there. I can hear him, and I know he’s trying to help, but I give these short answers and I don’t really engage. It feels like I can’t make myself care enough to get moving, even about things I know I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the things you used to enjoy, like walking on the beach or baking, do they still feel good or satisfying when you do them, or do they feel more flat or empty now?\n\nPatient: I guess when I do them… or, really, when I think about doing them, they feel kind of flat now. It’s hard to answer because I don’t actually get myself to do them much anymore. Like beach walks used to be something I would just go do, even if it was just for a little while, and I’d feel better after. Baking used to feel like there was a point to it, like I could focus on the steps and then have something nice at the end.\n\nNow it’s more like I think, “I could bake,” or “I should go outside,” and then I just sit there. I don’t really garden either, and I haven’t been writing in my journal much. It’s not that I do those things and hate them, exactly. It’s more like I can’t get the feeling started, if that makes sense. The satisfying part doesn’t seem strong enough to pull me toward it.\n\nAnd even with my husband, when he tries to talk with me or get me to do something, I mostly just sit there and give little answers. I know he’s trying, and I know these are things I used to care about, but it feels like I can’t make myself care enough to actually start.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the day, even when nothing specific has happened to make you feel that way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that, exactly. “Most of the day, most days” feels hard for me to answer because I’m not really measuring it that way in my head.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up and think, “I’m depressed,” and then feel that same sadness all day. Sometimes I feel heavy or kind of dull, like everything takes more effort than it should, but other times it’s more blank than sad. And sometimes I only notice something’s off when there’s no real reason for me to be sitting there doing nothing, but I still can’t seem to move into the day.\n\nSo… I don’t want to say yes if that means a clear depressed mood all day when nothing happened. I’m just not sure. I know I haven’t felt like myself, and a lot of days feel flat, but I can’t honestly pin it down as being down or depressed for most of the day nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately, even about small things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. It’s not just like forgetting where I put my phone or something normal like that. At work especially, it’s been… really embarrassing, and honestly it scared people, I think.\n\nI’m a waitress, so usually I can keep track of what I’m doing — table numbers, checks, who needs drinks, all that. But lately I’ll get stuck in the middle of service. Like I’ll be standing by the soda station with the cash drawer key in my hand, and instead of closing out checks or doing the next step, I’ll just kind of freeze there. And I’ll be saying table numbers under my breath, or repeating them, like I’m trying to line everything up in my head but it won’t connect.\n\nAnd it’s not just slow thinking. It’s like I do things out of order. I’ll start one task, then hold something I’m supposed to use for another task, and then I don’t finish either one. People will ask me, “Are you okay?” or tell me a table is waiting, and I know they’re talking to me, but it takes me a second to understand what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nSmall decisions feel big in those moments. Like closing a check should be simple — press the right buttons, take the payment, give the receipt — but I’ll just stand there like the steps got scrambled. I hate saying that because I know how to do my job. I’ve done it plenty. But lately my brain just… doesn’t move in the right order sometimes.\n\nDoctor: When you do things you used to enjoy, like walking on the beach or baking, do you still feel any pleasure or satisfaction from them, or do they feel more flat or empty now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s more flat now. Or maybe I should say I mostly don’t even get to the point of doing them, which is part of what bothers me.\n\nLike the beach is right there, and I used to be able to just go take a walk and feel at least a little clearer afterward. Baking too — I used to like the steps of it, measuring things out, waiting for it to come together. And gardening, writing in my journal… those were things that felt like mine. Now they just kind of sit there in my mind like chores I can’t start.\n\nWhen I think about doing them, there isn’t that pull anymore. It’s not like I do them and feel joyless every single time, because honestly I barely do them. It’s more like I can’t get myself to care enough to begin. I’ll think, “Maybe I should bake something,” and then I just sit there, and the thought passes, and nothing happens.\n\nAnd with my husband, it’s similar in a way. He’ll try to talk to me or get me to do something with him, and I know I should respond more, I know he’s trying, but I mostly just sit there and give little answers. It’s like the feeling that should make me want to connect or start something just doesn’t kick in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… I don’t really know how to answer the “while I’m doing it” part, because I’m hardly doing those things anymore. That’s the problem, I think.

Like with the beach walks, I can think about going. I’ll picture it, almost like, “That would probably be good for me,” because it used to be. But then I don’t actually get up and go. Baking is the same. Gardening, writing in my journal… they’re all things I used to just naturally start, and now they feel like they’re behind this wall. It’s not even that I try them and hate them every time. It’s more that I can’t get myself to begin.

And when my husband tries to talk with me or get me to do something, I know I should engage. I can tell he’s trying. But I mostly just sit there and give these small answers, and inside it’s like there’s no push to respond or care enough to start a real conversation. Not because I don’t love him or anything. It just feels like the part of me that would normally reach back toward him isn’t working right.

So if I do manage to do something, it doesn’t really feel satisfying the way it used to. Mostly it just feels… empty, or like I’m going through the idea of it without really being in it.

I mean, there are days when I sit around and don’t get much done, but I don’t always feel like, “Oh, I’m exhausted.” It’s more… I don’t know how to explain the difference.\n\nIf I rest, I’m still kind of the same afterward, but I can’t tell if that’s tiredness or just me not getting started. Like I’ll be on the couch for a while and then think I should get up and do something, and I just don’t. But I wouldn’t say I feel physically worn out all the time, not in a clear way. It’s hard for me to separate that from everything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, not just occasionally or in response to specific things happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, exactly. I wouldn’t want to say I’m depressed most of the day every day, because that sounds very specific, and I don’t know that I track it that clearly.\n\nThere are definitely times where I feel flat, or like I’m sitting under something heavy, but I don’t always feel sad in the way I think people mean when they say depressed. Sometimes I’m just… kind of blank. And then other times something will happen, like my husband asking me what I want for dinner or whether I want to go out, and I realize I don’t really have an answer, but I don’t know if that’s depressed or just me being stuck.\n\nSo I guess… I don’t know. I’ve noticed I’m not myself, but I can’t honestly say it’s a constant down feeling nearly every day. It’s more mixed up than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to enjoy, like walking on the beach or baking, don’t feel as rewarding or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s hard to say if they don’t feel good anymore because I barely even get to the point of doing them, you know? Like the beach walks — I used to like that feeling of just being outside and hearing the water and kind of letting my head settle. And baking, too, it used to feel kind of satisfying, like there was a reason to start and then something nice at the end.\n\nBut lately I just… don’t really do any of it. I don’t go for walks much, I don’t bake, I don’t really garden, and I haven’t been writing in my journal like I used to. I’ll think about it sometimes, like, “That would probably be good for me,” but then it feels like there’s no spark or pull to actually start. It’s not like I’m actively thinking, “I hate this now.” It’s more like it doesn’t reach me the same way.\n\nAnd when my husband tries to talk to me or encourage me, I mostly just sit there. I can hear him, and I know he’s trying to help, but I give these short answers and I don’t really engage. It feels like I can’t make myself care enough to get moving, even about things I know I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the things you used to enjoy, like walking on the beach or baking, do they still feel good or satisfying when you do them, or do they feel more flat or empty now?\n\nPatient: I guess when I do them… or, really, when I think about doing them, they feel kind of flat now. It’s hard to answer because I don’t actually get myself to do them much anymore. Like beach walks used to be something I would just go do, even if it was just for a little while, and I’d feel better after. Baking used to feel like there was a point to it, like I could focus on the steps and then have something nice at the end.\n\nNow it’s more like I think, “I could bake,” or “I should go outside,” and then I just sit there. I don’t really garden either, and I haven’t been writing in my journal much. It’s not that I do those things and hate them, exactly. It’s more like I can’t get the feeling started, if that makes sense. The satisfying part doesn’t seem strong enough to pull me toward it.\n\nAnd even with my husband, when he tries to talk with me or get me to do something, I mostly just sit there and give little answers. I know he’s trying, and I know these are things I used to care about, but it feels like I can’t make myself care enough to actually start.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the day, even when nothing specific has happened to make you feel that way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that, exactly. “Most of the day, most days” feels hard for me to answer because I’m not really measuring it that way in my head.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up and think, “I’m depressed,” and then feel that same sadness all day. Sometimes I feel heavy or kind of dull, like everything takes more effort than it should, but other times it’s more blank than sad. And sometimes I only notice something’s off when there’s no real reason for me to be sitting there doing nothing, but I still can’t seem to move into the day.\n\nSo… I don’t want to say yes if that means a clear depressed mood all day when nothing happened. I’m just not sure. I know I haven’t felt like myself, and a lot of days feel flat, but I can’t honestly pin it down as being down or depressed for most of the day nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately, even about small things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. It’s not just like forgetting where I put my phone or something normal like that. At work especially, it’s been… really embarrassing, and honestly it scared people, I think.\n\nI’m a waitress, so usually I can keep track of what I’m doing — table numbers, checks, who needs drinks, all that. But lately I’ll get stuck in the middle of service. Like I’ll be standing by the soda station with the cash drawer key in my hand, and instead of closing out checks or doing the next step, I’ll just kind of freeze there. And I’ll be saying table numbers under my breath, or repeating them, like I’m trying to line everything up in my head but it won’t connect.\n\nAnd it’s not just slow thinking. It’s like I do things out of order. I’ll start one task, then hold something I’m supposed to use for another task, and then I don’t finish either one. People will ask me, “Are you okay?” or tell me a table is waiting, and I know they’re talking to me, but it takes me a second to understand what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nSmall decisions feel big in those moments. Like closing a check should be simple — press the right buttons, take the payment, give the receipt — but I’ll just stand there like the steps got scrambled. I hate saying that because I know how to do my job. I’ve done it plenty. But lately my brain just… doesn’t move in the right order sometimes.\n\nDoctor: When you do things you used to enjoy, like walking on the beach or baking, do you still feel any pleasure or satisfaction from them, or do they feel more flat or empty now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s more flat now. Or maybe I should say I mostly don’t even get to the point of doing them, which is part of what bothers me.\n\nLike the beach is right there, and I used to be able to just go take a walk and feel at least a little clearer afterward. Baking too — I used to like the steps of it, measuring things out, waiting for it to come together. And gardening, writing in my journal… those were things that felt like mine. Now they just kind of sit there in my mind like chores I can’t start.\n\nWhen I think about doing them, there isn’t that pull anymore. It’s not like I do them and feel joyless every single time, because honestly I barely do them. It’s more like I can’t get myself to care enough to begin. I’ll think, “Maybe I should bake something,” and then I just sit there, and the thought passes, and nothing happens.\n\nAnd with my husband, it’s similar in a way. He’ll try to talk to me or get me to do something with him, and I know I should respond more, I know he’s trying, but I mostly just sit there and give little answers. It’s like the feeling that should make me want to connect or start something just doesn’t kick in.\n\nDoctor: When you actually do go for a walk on the beach or try baking something, do you feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction while you’re doing it, or does it feel empty and unenjoyable?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t really know how to answer the “while I’m doing it” part, because I’m hardly doing those things anymore. That’s the problem, I think.\n\nLike with the beach walks, I can think about going. I’ll picture it, almost like, “That would probably be good for me,” because it used to be. But then I don’t actually get up and go. Baking is the same. Gardening, writing in my journal… they’re all things I used to just naturally start, and now they feel like they’re behind this wall. It’s not even that I try them and hate them every time. It’s more that I can’t get myself to begin.\n\nAnd when my husband tries to talk with me or get me to do something, I know I should engage. I can tell he’s trying. But I mostly just sit there and give these small answers, and inside it’s like there’s no push to respond or care enough to start a real conversation. Not because I don’t love him or anything. It just feels like the part of me that would normally reach back toward him isn’t working right.\n\nSo if I do manage to do something, it doesn’t really feel satisfying the way it used to. Mostly it just feels… empty, or like I’m going through the idea of it without really being in it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I know I kind of already said this, but that’s the part that’s hard to explain. I don’t really go for the beach walks anymore, or bake, or garden, or write in my journal much at all. So it’s not like I have a lot of recent times where I can say, “Oh, I did it and it felt good,” or “I did it and it felt awful.” Mostly I just… don’t start.

Like I’ll think about going to the beach, and it should be easy — it’s not some huge plan — but I just sit there and it doesn’t turn into action. Same with baking. I can picture the ingredients, the counter, the steps, and normally that used to feel kind of comforting. Now it just feels like too much, or like there’s no reason to get up and do it.

And with my husband, when he tries to talk to me or get me to engage, I mostly just sit there. I’ll answer a little, but I don’t really reach back. It feels like I can’t get myself to care enough or start anything, even when I know I should. So if I do manage to do something, it’s pretty empty, but most of the time I don’t even get that far.

Like I used to go for walks on the beach, not even as a big thing, just to clear my head after work or on a day off. Like I’ll be on the couch for a while and then think I should get up and do something, and I just don’t. But I wouldn’t say I feel physically worn out all the time, not in a clear way. I’ve noticed I’m not myself, but I can’t honestly say it’s a constant down feeling nearly every day. It’s not like I’m actively thinking, “I hate this now.” It’s more like it doesn’t reach me the same way.\n\nAnd when my husband tries to talk to me or encourage me, I mostly just sit there. I can hear him, and I know he’s trying to help, but I give these short answers and I don’t really engage. It feels like I can’t make myself care enough to get moving, even about things I know I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the things you used to enjoy, like walking on the beach or baking, do they still feel good or satisfying when you do them, or do they feel more flat or empty now?\n\nPatient: I guess when I do them… or, really, when I think about doing them, they feel kind of flat now. It’s hard to answer because I don’t actually get myself to do them much anymore. Like beach walks used to be something I would just go do, even if it was just for a little while, and I’d feel better after. Baking used to feel like there was a point to it, like I could focus on the steps and then have something nice at the end.\n\nNow it’s more like I think, “I could bake,” or “I should go outside,” and then I just sit there. I don’t really garden either, and I haven’t been writing in my journal much. It’s not that I do those things and hate them, exactly. It’s more like I can’t get the feeling started, if that makes sense. The satisfying part doesn’t seem strong enough to pull me toward it.\n\nAnd even with my husband, when he tries to talk with me or get me to do something, I mostly just sit there and give little answers. I know he’s trying, and I know these are things I used to care about, but it feels like I can’t make myself care enough to actually start.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the day, even when nothing specific has happened to make you feel that way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that, exactly. “Most of the day, most days” feels hard for me to answer because I’m not really measuring it that way in my head.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up and think, “I’m depressed,” and then feel that same sadness all day. Sometimes I feel heavy or kind of dull, like everything takes more effort than it should, but other times it’s more blank than sad. And sometimes I only notice something’s off when there’s no real reason for me to be sitting there doing nothing, but I still can’t seem to move into the day.\n\nSo… I don’t want to say yes if that means a clear depressed mood all day when nothing happened. I’m just not sure. I know I haven’t felt like myself, and a lot of days feel flat, but I can’t honestly pin it down as being down or depressed for most of the day nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately, even about small things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. It’s not just like forgetting where I put my phone or something normal like that. At work especially, it’s been… really embarrassing, and honestly it scared people, I think.\n\nI’m a waitress, so usually I can keep track of what I’m doing — table numbers, checks, who needs drinks, all that. But lately I’ll get stuck in the middle of service. Like I’ll be standing by the soda station with the cash drawer key in my hand, and instead of closing out checks or doing the next step, I’ll just kind of freeze there. And I’ll be saying table numbers under my breath, or repeating them, like I’m trying to line everything up in my head but it won’t connect.\n\nAnd it’s not just slow thinking. It’s like I do things out of order. I’ll start one task, then hold something I’m supposed to use for another task, and then I don’t finish either one. People will ask me, “Are you okay?” or tell me a table is waiting, and I know they’re talking to me, but it takes me a second to understand what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nSmall decisions feel big in those moments. Like closing a check should be simple — press the right buttons, take the payment, give the receipt — but I’ll just stand there like the steps got scrambled. I hate saying that because I know how to do my job. I’ve done it plenty. But lately my brain just… doesn’t move in the right order sometimes.\n\nDoctor: When you do things you used to enjoy, like walking on the beach or baking, do you still feel any pleasure or satisfaction from them, or do they feel more flat or empty now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s more flat now. Or maybe I should say I mostly don’t even get to the point of doing them, which is part of what bothers me.\n\nLike the beach is right there, and I used to be able to just go take a walk and feel at least a little clearer afterward. Baking too — I used to like the steps of it, measuring things out, waiting for it to come together. And gardening, writing in my journal… those were things that felt like mine. Now they just kind of sit there in my mind like chores I can’t start.\n\nWhen I think about doing them, there isn’t that pull anymore. It’s not like I do them and feel joyless every single time, because honestly I barely do them. It’s more like I can’t get myself to care enough to begin. I’ll think, “Maybe I should bake something,” and then I just sit there, and the thought passes, and nothing happens.\n\nAnd with my husband, it’s similar in a way. He’ll try to talk to me or get me to do something with him, and I know I should respond more, I know he’s trying, but I mostly just sit there and give little answers. It’s like the feeling that should make me want to connect or start something just doesn’t kick in.\n\nDoctor: When you actually do go for a walk on the beach or try baking something, do you feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction while you’re doing it, or does it feel empty and unenjoyable?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t really know how to answer the “while I’m doing it” part, because I’m hardly doing those things anymore. That’s the problem, I think.\n\nLike with the beach walks, I can think about going. I’ll picture it, almost like, “That would probably be good for me,” because it used to be. But then I don’t actually get up and go. Baking is the same. Gardening, writing in my journal… they’re all things I used to just naturally start, and now they feel like they’re behind this wall. It’s not even that I try them and hate them every time. It’s more that I can’t get myself to begin.\n\nAnd when my husband tries to talk with me or get me to do something, I know I should engage. I can tell he’s trying. But I mostly just sit there and give these small answers, and inside it’s like there’s no push to respond or care enough to start a real conversation. Not because I don’t love him or anything. It just feels like the part of me that would normally reach back toward him isn’t working right.\n\nSo if I do manage to do something, it doesn’t really feel satisfying the way it used to. Mostly it just feels… empty, or like I’m going through the idea of it without really being in it.\n\nDoctor: When you actually do go for a walk on the beach or try baking something, do you feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction while you're doing it, or does it feel empty and unenjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I know I kind of already said this, but that’s the part that’s hard to explain. I don’t really go for the beach walks anymore, or bake, or garden, or write in my journal much at all. So it’s not like I have a lot of recent times where I can say, “Oh, I did it and it felt good,” or “I did it and it felt awful.” Mostly I just… don’t start.\n\nLike I’ll think about going to the beach, and it should be easy — it’s not some huge plan — but I just sit there and it doesn’t turn into action. Same with baking. I can picture the ingredients, the counter, the steps, and normally that used to feel kind of comforting. Now it just feels like too much, or like there’s no reason to get up and do it.\n\nAnd with my husband, when he tries to talk to me or get me to engage, I mostly just sit there. I’ll answer a little, but I don’t really reach back. It feels like I can’t get myself to care enough or start anything, even when I know I should. So if I do manage to do something, it’s pretty empty, but most of the time I don’t even get that far.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Right… I know you’re asking about when I actually do it, and I guess the honest answer is that I almost don’t get there anymore.

I don’t really go for the beach walks much now. I don’t bake, or garden, or write in my journal the way I used to. Those things used to feel like… not exactly exciting every second, but they had a kind of comfort to them. Like I could start them and then be in the middle of them and feel a little settled. Now it’s like they’re sitting on the other side of a door I can’t make myself open.

So if I do manage to do something, it doesn’t feel like much. It’s not satisfying in the way I remember. But most of the time it’s more that I can’t start at all. I’ll think, “I should go walk,” or “I could bake something,” and then I just stay where I am, and the thought sort of fades.

And with my husband, it’s kind of the same feeling. He’ll try to talk with me, and I know I should answer more or show interest, but I mostly just sit there. I give these little responses, and inside it feels like I can’t get myself to care enough or move toward anything, even though I know I should.

I wouldn’t want to say I’m depressed most of the day every day, because that sounds very specific, and I don’t know that I track it that clearly.\n\nThere are definitely times where I feel flat, or like I’m sitting under something heavy, but I don’t always feel sad in the way I think people mean when they say depressed. Sometimes I’m just… kind of blank. And then other times something will happen, like my husband asking me what I want for dinner or whether I want to go out, and I realize I don’t really have an answer, but I don’t know if that’s depressed or just me being stuck.\n\nSo I guess… I don’t know. I’ve noticed I’m not myself, but I can’t honestly say it’s a constant down feeling nearly every day. It’s more mixed up than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to enjoy, like walking on the beach or baking, don’t feel as rewarding or pleasurable as they used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s hard to say if they don’t feel good anymore because I barely even get to the point of doing them, you know? Like the beach walks — I used to like that feeling of just being outside and hearing the water and kind of letting my head settle. And baking, too, it used to feel kind of satisfying, like there was a reason to start and then something nice at the end.\n\nBut lately I just… don’t really do any of it. I don’t go for walks much, I don’t bake, I don’t really garden, and I haven’t been writing in my journal like I used to. I’ll think about it sometimes, like, “That would probably be good for me,” but then it feels like there’s no spark or pull to actually start. It’s not like I’m actively thinking, “I hate this now.” It’s more like it doesn’t reach me the same way.\n\nAnd when my husband tries to talk to me or encourage me, I mostly just sit there. I can hear him, and I know he’s trying to help, but I give these short answers and I don’t really engage. It feels like I can’t make myself care enough to get moving, even about things I know I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the things you used to enjoy, like walking on the beach or baking, do they still feel good or satisfying when you do them, or do they feel more flat or empty now?\n\nPatient: I guess when I do them… or, really, when I think about doing them, they feel kind of flat now. It’s hard to answer because I don’t actually get myself to do them much anymore. Like beach walks used to be something I would just go do, even if it was just for a little while, and I’d feel better after. Baking used to feel like there was a point to it, like I could focus on the steps and then have something nice at the end.\n\nNow it’s more like I think, “I could bake,” or “I should go outside,” and then I just sit there. I don’t really garden either, and I haven’t been writing in my journal much. It’s not that I do those things and hate them, exactly. It’s more like I can’t get the feeling started, if that makes sense. The satisfying part doesn’t seem strong enough to pull me toward it.\n\nAnd even with my husband, when he tries to talk with me or get me to do something, I mostly just sit there and give little answers. I know he’s trying, and I know these are things I used to care about, but it feels like I can’t make myself care enough to actually start.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the day, even when nothing specific has happened to make you feel that way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that, exactly. “Most of the day, most days” feels hard for me to answer because I’m not really measuring it that way in my head.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up and think, “I’m depressed,” and then feel that same sadness all day. Sometimes I feel heavy or kind of dull, like everything takes more effort than it should, but other times it’s more blank than sad. And sometimes I only notice something’s off when there’s no real reason for me to be sitting there doing nothing, but I still can’t seem to move into the day.\n\nSo… I don’t want to say yes if that means a clear depressed mood all day when nothing happened. I’m just not sure. I know I haven’t felt like myself, and a lot of days feel flat, but I can’t honestly pin it down as being down or depressed for most of the day nearly every day.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately, even about small things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. It’s not just like forgetting where I put my phone or something normal like that. At work especially, it’s been… really embarrassing, and honestly it scared people, I think.\n\nI’m a waitress, so usually I can keep track of what I’m doing — table numbers, checks, who needs drinks, all that. But lately I’ll get stuck in the middle of service. Like I’ll be standing by the soda station with the cash drawer key in my hand, and instead of closing out checks or doing the next step, I’ll just kind of freeze there. And I’ll be saying table numbers under my breath, or repeating them, like I’m trying to line everything up in my head but it won’t connect.\n\nAnd it’s not just slow thinking. It’s like I do things out of order. I’ll start one task, then hold something I’m supposed to use for another task, and then I don’t finish either one. People will ask me, “Are you okay?” or tell me a table is waiting, and I know they’re talking to me, but it takes me a second to understand what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nSmall decisions feel big in those moments. Like closing a check should be simple — press the right buttons, take the payment, give the receipt — but I’ll just stand there like the steps got scrambled. I hate saying that because I know how to do my job. I’ve done it plenty. But lately my brain just… doesn’t move in the right order sometimes.\n\nDoctor: When you do things you used to enjoy, like walking on the beach or baking, do you still feel any pleasure or satisfaction from them, or do they feel more flat or empty now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s more flat now. Or maybe I should say I mostly don’t even get to the point of doing them, which is part of what bothers me.\n\nLike the beach is right there, and I used to be able to just go take a walk and feel at least a little clearer afterward. Baking too — I used to like the steps of it, measuring things out, waiting for it to come together. And gardening, writing in my journal… those were things that felt like mine. Now they just kind of sit there in my mind like chores I can’t start.\n\nWhen I think about doing them, there isn’t that pull anymore. It’s not like I do them and feel joyless every single time, because honestly I barely do them. It’s more like I can’t get myself to care enough to begin. I’ll think, “Maybe I should bake something,” and then I just sit there, and the thought passes, and nothing happens.\n\nAnd with my husband, it’s similar in a way. He’ll try to talk to me or get me to do something with him, and I know I should respond more, I know he’s trying, but I mostly just sit there and give little answers. It’s like the feeling that should make me want to connect or start something just doesn’t kick in.\n\nDoctor: When you actually do go for a walk on the beach or try baking something, do you feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction while you’re doing it, or does it feel empty and unenjoyable?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t really know how to answer the “while I’m doing it” part, because I’m hardly doing those things anymore. That’s the problem, I think.\n\nLike with the beach walks, I can think about going. I’ll picture it, almost like, “That would probably be good for me,” because it used to be. But then I don’t actually get up and go. Baking is the same. Gardening, writing in my journal… they’re all things I used to just naturally start, and now they feel like they’re behind this wall. It’s not even that I try them and hate them every time. It’s more that I can’t get myself to begin.\n\nAnd when my husband tries to talk with me or get me to do something, I know I should engage. I can tell he’s trying. But I mostly just sit there and give these small answers, and inside it’s like there’s no push to respond or care enough to start a real conversation. Not because I don’t love him or anything. It just feels like the part of me that would normally reach back toward him isn’t working right.\n\nSo if I do manage to do something, it doesn’t really feel satisfying the way it used to. Mostly it just feels… empty, or like I’m going through the idea of it without really being in it.\n\nDoctor: When you actually do go for a walk on the beach or try baking something, do you feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction while you're doing it, or does it feel empty and unenjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I know I kind of already said this, but that’s the part that’s hard to explain. I don’t really go for the beach walks anymore, or bake, or garden, or write in my journal much at all. So it’s not like I have a lot of recent times where I can say, “Oh, I did it and it felt good,” or “I did it and it felt awful.” Mostly I just… don’t start.\n\nLike I’ll think about going to the beach, and it should be easy — it’s not some huge plan — but I just sit there and it doesn’t turn into action. Same with baking. I can picture the ingredients, the counter, the steps, and normally that used to feel kind of comforting. Now it just feels like too much, or like there’s no reason to get up and do it.\n\nAnd with my husband, when he tries to talk to me or get me to engage, I mostly just sit there. I’ll answer a little, but I don’t really reach back. It feels like I can’t get myself to care enough or start anything, even when I know I should. So if I do manage to do something, it’s pretty empty, but most of the time I don’t even get that far.\n\nDoctor: When you actually do go for a walk on the beach or try baking something, do you feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction while you're doing it, or does it feel empty and unenjoyable?\n\nPatient: Right… I know you’re asking about when I actually do it, and I guess the honest answer is that I almost don’t get there anymore.\n\nI don’t really go for the beach walks much now. I don’t bake, or garden, or write in my journal the way I used to. Those things used to feel like… not exactly exciting every second, but they had a kind of comfort to them. Like I could start them and then be in the middle of them and feel a little settled. Now it’s like they’re sitting on the other side of a door I can’t make myself open.\n\nSo if I do manage to do something, it doesn’t feel like much. It’s not satisfying in the way I remember. But most of the time it’s more that I can’t start at all. I’ll think, “I should go walk,” or “I could bake something,” and then I just stay where I am, and the thought sort of fades.\n\nAnd with my husband, it’s kind of the same feeling. He’ll try to talk with me, and I know I should answer more or show interest, but I mostly just sit there. I give these little responses, and inside it feels like I can’t get myself to care enough or move toward anything, even though I know I should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I haven’t really written it down or counted it.\n\nAnd with the other things you mentioned — sleep, appetite, concentration, guilt, feeling worthless — I’d have to think about each one. I don’t want to just say yes because you listed them. I guess I’ve mostly noticed the loss of interest part. The rest, I’m not sure I’ve paid enough attention to say.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in your appetite lately, like eating more or less than usual?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear change with appetite, honestly. Like, I can’t say, “Oh, I’m definitely eating way less,” or “I’m eating constantly.” It’s not something I’ve been tracking.

Some days I’ll realize I didn’t really think about food much, but then other days I eat more normally, so I don’t know if that counts as a change or if it’s just me not paying attention. If my husband asked me what I ate yesterday, I’d probably have to stop and really think through the whole day.

So… I guess my answer is I’m not sure. Nothing has stood out to me as a big appetite change.

It’s hard to answer because I don’t actually get myself to do them much anymore. Like beach walks used to be something I would just go do, even if it was just for a little while, and I’d feel better after. Baking used to feel like there was a point to it, like I could focus on the steps and then have something nice at the end.\n\nNow it’s more like I think, “I could bake,” or “I should go outside,” and then I just sit there. I know he’s trying, and I know these are things I used to care about, but it feels like I can’t make myself care enough to actually start.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most days, you feel down or depressed for most of the day, even when nothing specific has happened to make you feel that way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that, exactly. “Most of the day, most days” feels hard for me to answer because I’m not really measuring it that way in my head.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up and think, “I’m depressed,” and then feel that same sadness all day. Sometimes I feel heavy or kind of dull, like everything takes more effort than it should, but other times it’s more blank than sad. And sometimes I only notice something’s off when there’s no real reason for me to be sitting there doing nothing, but I still can’t seem to move into the day.\n\nSo… I don’t want to say yes if that means a clear depressed mood all day when nothing happened. I’m just not sure. Like I’ll be standing by the soda station with the cash drawer key in my hand, and instead of closing out checks or doing the next step, I’ll just kind of freeze there. Now they just kind of sit there in my mind like chores I can’t start.\n\nWhen I think about doing them, there isn’t that pull anymore. It’s not like I do them and feel joyless every single time, because honestly I barely do them. It’s more like I can’t get myself to care enough to begin. Not because I don’t love him or anything. It just feels like the part of me that would normally reach back toward him isn’t working right.\n\nSo if I do manage to do something, it doesn’t really feel satisfying the way it used to. Mostly it just feels… empty, or like I’m going through the idea of it without really being in it.\n\nDoctor: When you actually do go for a walk on the beach or try baking something, do you feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction while you're doing it, or does it feel empty and unenjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I know I kind of already said this, but that’s the part that’s hard to explain. I don’t really go for the beach walks anymore, or bake, or garden, or write in my journal much at all. So it’s not like I have a lot of recent times where I can say, “Oh, I did it and it felt good,” or “I did it and it felt awful.” Mostly I just… don’t start.\n\nLike I’ll think about going to the beach, and it should be easy — it’s not some huge plan — but I just sit there and it doesn’t turn into action. Same with baking. I can picture the ingredients, the counter, the steps, and normally that used to feel kind of comforting. Now it just feels like too much, or like there’s no reason to get up and do it.\n\nAnd with my husband, when he tries to talk to me or get me to engage, I mostly just sit there. So if I do manage to do something, it’s pretty empty, but most of the time I don’t even get that far.\n\nDoctor: When you actually do go for a walk on the beach or try baking something, do you feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction while you're doing it, or does it feel empty and unenjoyable?\n\nPatient: Right… I know you’re asking about when I actually do it, and I guess the honest answer is that I almost don’t get there anymore.\n\nI don’t really go for the beach walks much now. I don’t bake, or garden, or write in my journal the way I used to. Those things used to feel like… not exactly exciting every second, but they had a kind of comfort to them. Like I could start them and then be in the middle of them and feel a little settled. It’s like I do things out of order. I’ll start one task, then hold something I’m supposed to use for another task, and then I don’t finish either one. People will ask me, “Are you okay?” or tell me a table is waiting, and I know they’re talking to me, but it takes me a second to understand what I’m supposed to do next.\n\nSmall decisions feel big in those moments. Like closing a check should be simple — press the right buttons, take the payment, give the receipt — but I’ll just stand there like the steps got scrambled. I hate saying that because I know how to do my job. I’ve done it plenty. But lately my brain just… doesn’t move in the right order sometimes.\n\nDoctor: When you do things you used to enjoy, like walking on the beach or baking, do you still feel any pleasure or satisfaction from them, or do they feel more flat or empty now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s more flat now. Or maybe I should say I mostly don’t even get to the point of doing them, which is part of what bothers me.\n\nLike the beach is right there, and I used to be able to just go take a walk and feel at least a little clearer afterward. Baking too — I used to like the steps of it, measuring things out, waiting for it to come together. And gardening, writing in my journal… those were things that felt like mine. Now they just kind of sit there in my mind like chores I can’t start.\n\nWhen I think about doing them, there isn’t that pull anymore. It’s not like I do them and feel joyless every single time, because honestly I barely do them. It’s more like I can’t get myself to care enough to begin. I’ll think, “Maybe I should bake something,” and then I just sit there, and the thought passes, and nothing happens.\n\nAnd with my husband, it’s similar in a way. He’ll try to talk to me or get me to do something with him, and I know I should respond more, I know he’s trying, but I mostly just sit there and give little answers. It’s like the feeling that should make me want to connect or start something just doesn’t kick in.\n\nDoctor: When you actually do go for a walk on the beach or try baking something, do you feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction while you’re doing it, or does it feel empty and unenjoyable?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t really know how to answer the “while I’m doing it” part, because I’m hardly doing those things anymore. That’s the problem, I think.\n\nLike with the beach walks, I can think about going. I’ll picture it, almost like, “That would probably be good for me,” because it used to be. But then I don’t actually get up and go. Baking is the same. Gardening, writing in my journal… they’re all things I used to just naturally start, and now they feel like they’re behind this wall. It’s not even that I try them and hate them every time. It’s more that I can’t get myself to begin.\n\nAnd when my husband tries to talk with me or get me to do something, I know I should engage. I can tell he’s trying. But I mostly just sit there and give these small answers, and inside it’s like there’s no push to respond or care enough to start a real conversation. Not because I don’t love him or anything. It just feels like the part of me that would normally reach back toward him isn’t working right.\n\nSo if I do manage to do something, it doesn’t really feel satisfying the way it used to. Mostly it just feels… empty, or like I’m going through the idea of it without really being in it.\n\nDoctor: When you actually do go for a walk on the beach or try baking something, do you feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction while you're doing it, or does it feel empty and unenjoyable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I know I kind of already said this, but that’s the part that’s hard to explain. I don’t really go for the beach walks anymore, or bake, or garden, or write in my journal much at all. So it’s not like I have a lot of recent times where I can say, “Oh, I did it and it felt good,” or “I did it and it felt awful.” Mostly I just… don’t start.\n\nLike I’ll think about going to the beach, and it should be easy — it’s not some huge plan — but I just sit there and it doesn’t turn into action. Same with baking. I can picture the ingredients, the counter, the steps, and normally that used to feel kind of comforting. Now it just feels like too much, or like there’s no reason to get up and do it.\n\nAnd with my husband, when he tries to talk to me or get me to engage, I mostly just sit there. I’ll answer a little, but I don’t really reach back. It feels like I can’t get myself to care enough or start anything, even when I know I should. So if I do manage to do something, it’s pretty empty, but most of the time I don’t even get that far.\n\nDoctor: When you actually do go for a walk on the beach or try baking something, do you feel any sense of pleasure or satisfaction while you're doing it, or does it feel empty and unenjoyable?\n\nPatient: Right… I know you’re asking about when I actually do it, and I guess the honest answer is that I almost don’t get there anymore.\n\nI don’t really go for the beach walks much now. I don’t bake, or garden, or write in my journal the way I used to. Those things used to feel like… not exactly exciting every second, but they had a kind of comfort to them. Like I could start them and then be in the middle of them and feel a little settled. Now it’s like they’re sitting on the other side of a door I can’t make myself open.\n\nSo if I do manage to do something, it doesn’t feel like much. It’s not satisfying in the way I remember. But most of the time it’s more that I can’t start at all. I’ll think, “I should go walk,” or “I could bake something,” and then I just stay where I am, and the thought sort of fades.\n\nAnd with my husband, it’s kind of the same feeling. He’ll try to talk with me, and I know I should answer more or show interest, but I mostly just sit there. I give these little responses, and inside it feels like I can’t get myself to care enough or move toward anything, even though I know I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you had a period of time lasting at least two weeks where you felt down or experienced a loss of interest in most activities, and during that time, did you also have other symptoms like trouble sleeping, changes in appetite, trouble concentrating, or feeling guilty or worthless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I know I’ve had stretches where I feel flat and not interested in things, like I’ve been describing, but when you say “at least two weeks” and then all the other things together, I don’t know if I’ve been keeping track in that way.\n\nIt’s more like the days blur together. I can tell something is off because I’m not doing the things I used to do, and I’m not really reaching for anything. But if you ask me, “Was there a specific two-week period where this symptom and that symptom were all happening?” I don’t know. I haven’t really written it down or counted it.\n\nAnd with the other things you mentioned — sleep, appetite, concentration, guilt, feeling worthless — I’d have to think about each one. I don’t want to just say yes because you listed them. I guess I’ve mostly noticed the loss of interest part. The rest, I’m not sure I’ve paid enough attention to say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite lately, like eating more or less than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear change with appetite, honestly. Like, I can’t say, “Oh, I’m definitely eating way less,” or “I’m eating constantly.” It’s not something I’ve been tracking.\n\nSome days I’ll realize I didn’t really think about food much, but then other days I eat more normally, so I don’t know if that counts as a change or if it’s just me not paying attention. If my husband asked me what I ate yesterday, I’d probably have to stop and really think through the whole day.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I’m not sure. Nothing has stood out to me as a big appetite change.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
